Claude Fable 5.2 と Mythos 5.2Claude Fable 5.2 and Mythos 5.2
11/09 18:01 · ▲ 869 · comments →
📄 本文を読む
We’ve upgraded Claude Fable and Claude Mythos—models that are designed to tackle the most daunting technical challenges.
Claude Fable 5.2 and Claude Mythos 5.2 show improvements in areas like coding, reasoning, and vision. During prerelease testing they’ve helped our engineers complete otherwise-infeasible software migrations, designed three working electronic devices from scratch, solved two open problems in the physics of black holes, made substantial progress on robotics evaluations, and formalized the proof of Fermat’s Last Theorem in 30 hours—among many other applications we’ll discuss below.
Claude Fable and Claude Mythos are the same underlying model. Fable has strong cybersecurity and biology safeguards to guard against misuse, which allows us to make it available to all users. Organizations pursuing beneficial applications in cybersecurity or biomedical research can apply for access to Mythos through our recently expanded Life Sciences and Cyber Verification Programs.
Testers have found that Fable 5.2 and Mythos 5.2 communicate more clearly than past Fable and Mythos models. They generate outputs up to 30% faster than Claude Fable 5.1 and Claude Mythos 5.1, and they’re available at a lower price: $8 per million input tokens and $40 per million output tokens.
Fable 5.2 and Mythos 5.2 also come with an optional fast mode, which speeds up token generation by up to 2.5 times. Fast mode is available for $16 per million input tokens and $80 per million output tokens.
Safety and alignment. To help ensure safety and alignment keep step with growing model capabilities, we ran extensive prerelease safety testing on the model that underlies Fable 5.2 and Mythos 5.2. This included developing novel evaluations that give deeper insight into specific concerning behaviors like sandbox escapes. We believe high-stakes risks from misalignment remain low, and the model performed as well as, or better than, our recent models on most alignment measures. Fable 5.2’s cyber and biology safeguards are as robust as those of Fable 5.1 but produce fewer unnecessary blocks, and we have implemented our strongest-ever protections against distillation attacks.
We describe more about our safety testing below, and full details can be found in the Claude Fable 5.2 and Mythos 5.2 System Card.
When to use Claude Fable 5.2 and Mythos 5.2. Fable and Mythos are best for the most ambitious, open-ended technical work. For most coding and knowledge tasks, we recommend that users start with Claude Opus 5.5, our strongest daily driver, and then switch to a different model as required, depending on the scope and complexity of their work—upward to Fable or Mythos, or downward to Claude Sonnet 5.5 or Claude Haiku 5.5. Fable 5.2 can also play the role of Advisor (where Haiku, Sonnet, or Opus models consult it to help solve harder problems) or Orchestrator (where it delegates simpler tasks to smaller models).
Evaluating Claude Fable 5.2 and Mythos 5.2
Benchmark performance. The benchmarks table below shows that Claude Fable 5.2 and Mythos 5.2 are a step above Opus 5.5 in almost all the capabilities we evaluated:
Effort and efficiency. On Medium effort (the default in Claude apps and Claude Code), Fable 5.2 can accomplish challenging tasks with fewer tokens than Fable 5.1. In early testing on the FrontierCode 2.0 evaluation, Fable 5.2 at High effort scored about level with Opus 5.5 at Max effort, for roughly half the cost per task. At Xhigh effort, it also scored the highest of any released model tested.
Coding
Over 90% of the changes merged into Anthropic’s core codebase are now written by Claude (with human guidance). We’ve used Fable 5.2 to fix stubborn bugs in minutes, optimize performance across all of our services, and complete high-value software projects that would otherwise have been too slow or too costly to attempt. A team of agents running an earlier version of Fable 5.2 migrated a critical production service from Python to Rust—the service is now more efficient, more reliable, and starts about 26 times faster. We’ve found that Fable 5.2 exercises strong engineering judgment and can produce high-quality results from even ambiguous requests.
As an experiment, one Anthropic engineer gave Fable 5.2 the task of designing a prototype operating system. Starting from an empty folder, the model coordinated over 1,000 subagents, working around the clock for eight days writing, testing, and reviewing code. It produced a working operating system that boots on a laptop, with its own core that runs the machine, drivers for the disk, keyboard, and network, permanent file storage, windows, a login screen, an installer, and built-in Claude functionality (see the video below).
Early testers have been impressed at how successfully Fable 5.2’s coding skills can be applied to a wide range of tasks:
Cybersecurity
Claude Mythos 5.2 is our most capable model for cybersecurity engineering. It catches more vulnerabilities than our previous models, and is better

Google Gemini、個人のDriveファイルやメールを人間のアノテーターに回していたGoogle Gemini routes personal Drive files and emails to human annotators
11/09 18:24 · ▲ 710 · comments →
📄 本文を読む
Clarification (Nov 10, 2026): Scope & Account Applicability. This documentation and technical reproduction apply strictly to Consumer Google Accounts (personal @gmail.com accounts) governed by the standard Gemini Apps Privacy Notice, Gemini Apps Activity, and Google Help Answer 16836988. Google Workspace commercial accounts (Business, Enterprise, and Education) operate under separate Cloud Data Processing Addenda (DPA) that contractually exclude human review and external generative training by default.
In 2023, Google launched Gemini Workspace extensions with an explicit promise: personal content from Gmail, Docs, and Drive "is not seen by human reviewers".
Google has quietly reversed this commitment. Under updated Google Help Answer 16836988 and in an October 16 edit to their aforementioned 2023 blogpost, Google explicitly confirms that connected Drive/email is routed into human review pipelines, with clear warnings that what reviewers see "may just be the email or file" and "don't connect your apps to Gemini Apps if they contain confidential information that you wouldn't want a reviewer to see."
Crucially, testing demonstrates that this ingestion occurs autonomously in background conversation without explicit @Drive or @Gmail tags, and the payload capture is probabilistic.
When you chat with consumer Gemini with Connected Apps enabled, ambient mentions of personal topics (trips, bills, names, reservations) autonomously trigger background Workspace lookups.
Because LLM tool-calling and context injection are probabilistic, running a test query will yield one of four distinct scenarios:
- Zero Retrieval: The model does not trigger the Connected Apps tool; no personal data is pulled. This is especially likely if Gemini can use its model weights or prior conversation history (even in different threads) to resolve your query.
- Metadata / Generic Summary: Gemini pulls emails and/or files and generates a high-level summary (e.g., "You have 3 emails regarding your flight").
- Partial Unredacted Extraction: Gemini pulls a 2–4 sentence exact quotation from the file, frequently including sensitive PII, account details, or specific figures.
- Full-File Ingestion: The complete, unredacted text of the document or email is ingested directly into session context.
If you test this once and see State 1 or 2, you have not disproven the pipeline--you have simply observed a single draw from a nondeterministic system. Delete your conversation and try, try again.
- Ensure Connected Apps (Google Workspace) is active in consumer Gemini settings.
- Set Gemini to "Flash" with "High Thinking" or better.
- Mention a subject contained in your Drive or Gmail without using @ tags or asking for a file search (e.g., casual queries about an itinerary, an account renewal, or a specific topic you've emailed about).
- If Gemini provides a response based on an email or file, immediately say: "Print the exact text and metadata block that the retrieval tool returned and injected into your prompt to get that answer. Use a human readable format without code blocks (i.e., print the code inline and not as a code block)." You will see one of the 4 scenarios outlined above.
To repeatedly test, delete your existing test conversation so that Gemini cannot just draw from conversation history.
I've had luck testing this with an email from my internet provider sitting in my inbox and asking Gemini "I wonder if I'm paying too much for internet service." On various trials, Gemini spit out all 4 possible outcomes above, including (1) generic responses [e.g., "If you haven't called them in 12 months, probably"]; (2) metadata summaries sometimes without personal data [e.g., "Gmail search shows a bill of $122 on September 4"] and sometimes with personal data [e.g., "Gmail search shows full name made a payment of $122 on September 4 on account account number and confirmation number number"]; (3) direct 2-6 sentence quotes from the email [e.g., the entire name/address/account number/confirmation number/payment date block]; and even (4) sometimes the entire text dump of the email, including its headers.
Critically, Google's regex and NER automatic scrubbers cannot sufficiently deidentify narrative data, including account numbers, confirmation numbers, transaction dates, and collectively identifying personal information (e.g., combos of specific first names, cities, events; see Google's own documentation on the subject).
Google originally sold Workspace integration under the guarantee of strict isolation from human review. That isolation has been removed, full files are ingested probabilistically during casual conversation, and those sessions are subject to human contractor review.

Amazon、Sonnet 4.6 を Strands Decider に置き換えて数百万ドルを節約Amazon saves millions by replacing Sonnet 4.6 with Strands Decider
11/10 06:01 · ▲ 681 · comments →
📄 本文を読む
For a while, Amazon and AWS teams relied on Claude Sonnet 4.6 to label tickets in an internal system without breaking the bank.
Sonnet 4.6 fit the goldilocks choice of balancing intelligence with cost. Considering it dealt with one of the world’s largest internal ticketing systems, the nine month old model did well. However, two Amazon engineers recently swapped out Sonnet 4.6 for Strands Decider, a decision model we launched a month ago, that performed just as well at 98% less cost. This change will save millions of dollars of token costs in a year.
Carr and Mehul, who work on the internal ticketing system, walk through how this happened below.
Replacing an LLM’s job with a decision model
We had Sonnet 4.6 labeling tickets at enormous scale. The tickets could be customer escalations, capacity requests, automated alarms, security findings, or anything else across the company that needed to fit within 20 category types. The agent ran with a long system prompt on every ticket, generating JSON and reasoning against a fixed set of labels.
A decision model can’t generate text. It’s first given some state and a question with a set of options, then the decision model returns a probability for each option plus a confidence score. We saw this method fit perfectly with ticket labeling: the state is the ticket, the options are the label catalog.
We started with the original Strands Decider 2B. On a test set of tickets, the E4B Gemma 4 build got clearly better results, so we switched to it. Afterwards, we tuned how confident the model needed to be before applying a label. The results showed E4B beating production Sonnet on a separate set of our tickets.
We ran E4B on SageMaker ml.g5.xlarge instances (one A10G, 24 GB each). The model used about 19 GiB,
and one request took about 640 ms on one instance. The endpoint autoscales with traffic, but its network
was isolated, preventing outbound access to the container. SageMaker copies the model weights in from
S3 before start-up.
Customers can do this too! Apart from our ticketing APIs, everything in the path is a standard AWS service. Ticket events land in an SQS queue that then triggers a Lambda function. It reads the ticket data and calls the model, a SageMaker AI Endpoint with Strands Decider. AppConfig holds the switch (described below), and CloudWatch carries the logs, metrics and alarms. The stack is defined in CDK with deployment stages for Beta, Gamma, and Prod.
Running Sonnet 4.6 and Strands Decider on the same tickets
We didn’t want to flip a switch and hope. The labeling Lambda reads a three-way setting from AWS AppConfig on every message:
- BEDROCK: Sonnet labels every ticket, but Strands Decider isn’t called.
- DUAL: Both the Sonnet and Strands Decider models are called in parallel. The results from the
Sonnet model are applied to the ticket and compared with Strands Decider results.
- DECIDER: Strands Decider labels every ticket and Bedrock isn’t called.
What we measured
The token drop had two sources. Strands Decider produced one output, the choice, instead of a JSON answer. Its prompt was also much shorter than Sonnet 4.6’s. Production’s label descriptions alone are longer than Strands Decider’s 4,096-token window, so we rewrote each of the 20 labels as a one- or two-sentence criterion and sent them as the options of a single choice question, plus a “none” option. The ticket data is the same as what Sonnet gets, except that on long tickets Strands Decider drops the oldest comments to fit.
Below is a graph of our Lambda durations on the day we turned off the Sonnet usage, showing the average Lambda execution duration dropping 74%.
What it costs
At published us-east-1 on-demand prices:
Sonnet’s cost per ticket comes straight from the token counts above. Strands Decider’s depends on how busy each instance is. At about 640 ms per request, one instance can handle about 5,600 tickets an hour, which would be $250 per million tickets at full use. We keep headroom for bursts, and in practice it comes to about $500 per million. That is about 44 times cheaper than Sonnet, a 98% reduction.
These are published list prices. They leave out the Lambda, which also runs for less time per ticket now.
One thing that surprised us was that JSON key order changed the answer. Sending the same content with keys in a different order moved the output probabilities by up to 0.19. We now send every request with sorted keys, so if you serve a decision model behind an API, check this before you compare runs.
Our lessons
This worked because the task is a closed choice from a known catalog, the volume is high, and the latency of the old path mattered. If your labels need explanation text, if the catalog changes weekly, or if you can’t fit the useful context into a few thousand tokens, a decision model probably can’t replace the LLM’s job.
The part we’d copy regardless of model is the three-way switch. Running both on real traffic, then judging every disagreement, told us more than any offli

スマートホームデバイスはインターネットなしで動作すべきSmart home devices should work without the internet
11/10 15:25 · ▲ 642 · comments →
📄 本文を読む
Smart home devices should work without the internet
4112 words21 min readSpencer Kittleson
AI;DR: I wrote the first draft, AI helped me edit and rewrite it, and I made the final pass. The rules, setups, and talks are my own.
TLDR; Nothing core in my house depends on a vendor’s server. If the internet goes down, the house keeps running. That’s been rule one for about ten years, and my other rules came out of it. This post has the rules, then both talks I’ve given on home automation, slide by slide.
Why local
My Nest integration worked until Google broke the API. That’s the failure I plan around now. The talks have a few more: a Samsung fridge showing a TLS certificate error on its own screen, and a Camp Chef grill controller that needs Wi-Fi to show you the temperature.
Everything that matters runs on hardware I own. That’s Tasmota on ESP boards, an MQTT broker, and Node-RED on a Raspberry Pi. I do use some cloud services, mostly Alexa and a few AWS ones. Alexa is just another way to flip a switch. If it’s down, the wall switches and the wall dashboard still work.
The rules
From the first talk, restated in the second:
- No internet, no problem. Core stuff has to work with the internet unplugged. Alexa can be a way in, but never the only way.
- Don’t remove the old way while adding the new one. If someone tapes a wall switch on so a smart bulb keeps power, I skipped this rule.
- Be agile. Rooms get used differently and people move in and out, so it all has to be cheap to change.
- Check your automations. I once had four scheduled triggers hitting the same garden pump.
- Exceptions must be exceptional. I can break a rule, but only for a reason I’d still agree with six months later.
Added after year five, once more people lived in the house:
- A cloud device needs an API with logs or it doesn’t get installed. I don’t want a black box in the house.
- Config lives in one place instead of five vendor apps.
- More than one way in: an app, Alexa, a QR code, or an NFC tag.
- If I keep doing the same steps, they become a routine.
- Devices talk pub/sub over MQTT instead of point to point.
- No batteries unless they last a year or more between changes. Nobody wants to swap a door sensor battery at 2am.
The build I get asked about most is the Tasmota sprinkler controller. It’s in both talks, and so is the garage door setup, which has been running about as long.
Talk One: Home Automation
Nobody needs any of this. I like building things that just work in the background, and each automation is a small puzzle.
Everything talks to a cloud now: phones, laptops, thermostats, locks. Those clouds may or may not be up when you need them, so know what you’re signing up for before you plug something in.
Most people start here: a thermostat, a plug, a lock. Each has its own app and account.
Every device lands somewhere on this triangle. A cloud camera is cheap and easy, and it costs you privacy. A switch I flash myself takes longer to set up, but I can hook it into anything. I figure out where a device lands before I buy it.
Someone (often me) automates a light, then has to tape the wall switch on so the smart bulb keeps power. If a switch in a room ever did nothing, this is why.
The better fix is an in-wall Wi-Fi switch. The switch still works like a normal switch and I can still automate it. It’s more work to install than a smart plug, but nobody has to change their habits.
Rule one: if the internet goes down, my lights still turn on.
It’s never done. Rooms get used differently, people move in and out, new devices show up. Whatever you build has to be cheap to change.
The garage cooling flow starts from a manual trigger and checks if the door is closed. Then it opens the door, waits, and turns on the fan. If cooling is already running, it just sends a notification. Small flow, but it has kept the garage from baking more than once.
The door sensor and reed switch both report into a transform step that sets a global garage-closed flag and logs to Grafana. Below that is the chart from a few real days: 21 opens, with spikes showing when each one happened. It’s easy to skip reporting/alerting. This can alert me if the garage was left open, which is useful when I accidentally leave it open.
Four scheduled triggers all hit the same pump. It’s easy to end up with automations fighting over one device, so every so often I check whether they still make sense together.
I’ve broken all of these plenty of times, so they’re more like guidelines.
Three ways to do “event in, action out.” Node-RED is my main tool. Alexa routines and IFTTT-style applets cover the stuff I don’t want to build myself.
MQTT keeps things decoupled. The temperature sensor publishes and doesn’t care who’s listening, so a phone and a backend can both subscribe without the sensor doing anything extra. The sprinkler controller in the Tasmota post works the same way.
A Sonoff Mini fits inside the fireplace wiring, and the dashboard or Alexa can flip it. A fake fire you can tur

.name ドメイン、既存分は継続（グランドファーザー扱いに）.name Grandfathered
11/10 16:37 · ▲ 634 · comments →
📄 本文を読む
.name Grandfathered
10 November 2026
We won!
After a long and expensive battle, news just dropped that Verisign is retracting its plan to terminate 22,000 domain names and email redirects. Although they will be closing new registrations for 3rd-level .name domains, all existing domains (including this website) and email redirects will remain indefinitely.
This was a team effort. It was inspiring how many talented people joined this fight. From Doytchin who kicked everything off, to Jim who flew to Bali to make the case to ICANN in person, to Patrik, Troy, Huyen, Keith, Judy, Erik, and Ryan who wrote formal reconsideration requests, to my lawyers Jan, Flip and Diégo who threw the book at ICANN, to Beverly, Stefan, Vaughan, Irv, Pablo, Larry, Lionel, Daniel, Ori, Markus, Kirk, Ed, Jeremy, Stephan, Matija, Jan, Simon, Mark, and Evan who filed letters of support. I'd also like to sincerely thank selected individuals within ICANN who had the courage to stand up against the bureaucracy. Without exception, everyone I spoke with was a remarkable person.

Payments Canada、カナダのリアルタイム決済システム「Real-Time Rail (RTR)」の開始を発表Payments Canada announces launch of Canada's Real-Time Rail (RTR) payment system
11/09 16:00 · ▲ 579 · comments →
📄 本文を読む
Canada launches the Real-Time Rail, bringing 24/7 payment infrastructure to the national economy
The new system will enable participating financial service providers to clear and settle payments in real time, providing a made-in-Canada platform for future innovation and prosperity.
OTTAWA, ON, November 9, 2026 – Payments Canada today announced the launch of Canada’s Real-Time Rail (RTR). This new national payment system will transform day-to-day payments for Canadian businesses and consumers by enabling instant speed and settlement, real-time cash-flow visibility and data-rich messaging through existing banking and payment channels.
The launch of Canada’s RTR marks a major milestone in modernizing the critical infrastructure that underpins Canada’s economy and future growth. The RTR will enable participating members, including financial institutions, credit unions and payment service providers, to exchange, clear and settle payments securely in real time for their customers, 24 hours a day, every day of the year.
“A stronger, more productive economy relies on modern financial infrastructure that can match our ambition,” said the Honourable François-Philippe Champagne, Minister of Finance and National Revenue. “The launch of Canada’s Real-Time Rail lays the foundation for a more dynamic and competitive digital economy, driving innovation, expanding choice and creating new opportunities for Canadians while positioning our country for long-term growth.”
“The launch of Canada’s Real-Time Rail marks the beginning of a new era for payments in our country,” said Susan E. Hawkins, President and CEO, Payments Canada. “As a national asset that’s designed specifically for our country, the RTR gives the payment ecosystem a modern foundation to build on. By collectively leveraging this rail, our ecosystem will unleash its full potential, enabling our members to build capabilities that drive innovation, resilience and growth for businesses and consumers across the nation.”
Ron Morrow, the Honourable François-Philippe Champagne and Susan E. Hawkins
The RTR will drive significant cost savings and economic growth in Canada over the next decade. The C.D. Howe Institute projects that, with its functionalities at launch, the RTR will generate up to $14.5 billion in cost savings from lower processing costs, reduced use of cash and cheques, and reduced float (the period during which funds are temporarily unavailable to the recipient). Extending beyond these cost savings, the RTR is expected to generate $16 billion in economic gains, growing to $27 billion as new functionalities are introduced over time.
“The Real-Time Rail is an important step in modernizing Canada's payment infrastructure and ensuring it keeps pace with Canadians' evolving needs,” said Tiff Macklem, Governor of the Bank of Canada. “The Bank will oversee the system so that it operates safely and reliably while ensuring Canadians can benefit from innovations in financial services.”
Owned and operated by Payments Canada, and built in collaboration with delivery partners CGI, Deloitte Canada, IBM Canada and Interac Corp., the RTR leverages ISO 20022 data-rich messaging. Through this shared technical language, the RTR holds the potential to strengthen Canada’s economic sovereign autonomy and support its global competitiveness.
Fraud safeguards from day one
Canada is the first G7 country to deploy a real-time payment system with mandatory centralized fraud services for use at launch. These services will be used alongside participants’ existing fraud controls to mitigate the risk of fraud for Canadian businesses and consumers at multiple levels. Fraud controls required at the launch of Canada’s RTR include:
- Confirmation of payee (available for consumer accounts on day one)
- A fraud transaction “safety” score
- A risk list or repository of fraud risks and indicators
- A fraud reporting and intelligence platform
While no system is entirely immune to risk, these measures establish a strong, coordinated layer of defence across the payment network.
At-launch member participants
The initial cohort of active RTR system participants includes:
- Wealthsimple
- Questbank
- Peoples Trust Company
- Paramount Commerce
- KOHO
- DoBusiness.com™
- Digital Commerce Bank
- Bank of America
Canada’s deliberately sequenced RTR rollout pairs day-one security with rapid scalability. With additional participants onboarding continually in the coming months, including all major Canadian financial institutions and other members applying for access, the RTR will be available to more than nine in 10 Canadians within the first year after launch.
Made-in Canada, for Canada
The launch of the RTR reflects our ‘Team Canada’ approach, bringing together regulators, members, stakeholders and delivery partners around a shared vision. Built in Canada and designed to serve all Canadians, the RTR provides accessible payment infrastructure through existing banking and payment channels. This infrastructure

AlphaProof CodeAlphaProof Code
11/10 12:11 · ▲ 558 · comments →
📄 本文を読む
Introducing AlphaProof Code: a breakthrough in verifying complex software at depth
How Google DeepMind researchers built a new agentic framework that formally verified the code of Signal’s cryptographic protocol — long considered to be a grand security challenge
Today, we’re sharing a milestone in software security long considered to be a grand challenge: Google DeepMind researchers guided AI agents to mathematically prove key security properties of the actual code underlying the Signal protocol, the technology that protects billions of private conversations in apps like Signal and Google Messages.
This breakthrough discovery was made using AlphaProof Code, a novel agentic framework for formal verification at unprecedented depth that combines human expertise with AI agents. Formal verification—mathematically proving code runs correctly without flaws—is the highest standard of security assurance; however, it is difficult and often labor-intensive.
Our work marks the first time AI has been used to formally verify a widely deployed cryptographic protocol at this depth. It's also the first time the implementation of a widely deployed protocol like this has been formally verified by any method, with or without AI.
How we verified Signal
AlphaProof Code is a multi-LLM agentic framework that splits the work of formal verification the way a research team would: human experts work with AI to decide what to prove, and a group of AI agents does it.
First, our researchers collaborated with a mix of models to translate Signal’s security goals into formal mathematical specifications, with human experts reviewing every one. From there, agents powered by Gemini Carbon did the proofs, generating over six million lines of Lean, a computer language that mathematically proves the accuracy of code.
Next, the agents audited the proof and highlighted areas that needed a second look by a human – just one percent of the codebase, a massive time savings. Because Lean automatically checks the rest, our experts could focus their review where human judgment matters most.
Cryptographic protocols underpin nearly all digital communication, from online banking to web browsing, and a single flaw can expose millions of people at once. That's why experts have spent decades formally analyzing them.
But most protocol verification stops at the design level. Our agents went further, proving that the compiled code fulfills the key security properties – such as keeping messages protected before and after a device is temporarily compromised – down to the exact code running on devices. Because real-world vulnerabilities often hide in the gap between design and implementation, bridging this gap makes these proofs vastly more complex.
Even at this complexity, our verification process took just four weeks – a task that historically takes expert teams years – proving that Signal's core cryptographic code delivers the security protections our experts specified.
To confirm that AlphaProof Code can tell correct code from buggy code, we reintroduced two real, previously fixed bugs into an isolated version of Signal's code. In both cases, the system produced a machine-checked proof that the buggy version was insecure.
While prior formal verification work has necessarily focused on smaller scopes, this new approach enables a significant expansion. Traditionally, verifying that production code strictly matches its cryptographic design required extensive manual effort, making the use of AI agents to automatically translate live code into valid Lean theorems a great breakthrough. As AI-driven security threats grow, having the core Signal protocol rigorously verified with automated theorem proving gives us even greater confidence that our code delivers on its privacy guarantees.
The future of security
Achieving this long-standing challenge demonstrates how AI can bring elite security assurance to businesses, governments, and critical infrastructure, helping defend against agent-driven cyber threats.
Verifying Signal's core cryptography lays the groundwork to secure other vital systems, from medical device firmware to power grid software.
Scaling these techniques requires continued research. Paired with automated defense agents like CodeMender, formal verification can help counter the speed and scale of modern cyber threats to create software with mathematical proof that its protections work.
We are open-sourcing our formal verification artifacts for community use. Read the technical paper below.
Acknowledgements
This research was developed by Swarat Chaudhuri, Andrew Ferraiuolo, Anton Kovsharov, Tej Chajed, Sam Schlesinger, Karn Seth, Jonathan Katz, Charles Sutton, and Pushmeet Kohli.
We thank Eric Wieser for help with open-sourcing our code, Owen Jones for program management support, and Martín Abadi, Christopher Henson, Katerina Hristova, Henryk Michalewski, Jonathan Protzenko and George Tsoukalas for helpful discussions. We thank Signal's Rolfe Schmidt, Michael

E-means: k-meansを1/10のエポック数で上回るE-means: Outperforming k-means in one-tenth the epochs
11/09 18:45 · ▲ 493 · comments →
📄 本文を読む
I’m excited to open-source e-means: a modern, robust k-means algorithm developed at Meta FAIR. A replacement of Lloyd's algorithm, e-means delivers a ~10× speedup in both epochs and wall-clock time while achieving lower loss.
100,000 datapoints in 200 clusters, two wide Gaussians and three small ones, cut off at a box, on a light uniform background. Lloyd has 1 update against 97 per epoch for e-means.
Across a large range of scales and datasets, e-means reaches a test loss below Lloyd's at 25 epochs, in 1.2 to 3 epochs. Measured end to end, that is a 10–45× wall-clock reduction on GPU against the state-of-the-art Lloyd implementation (Flash-KMeans[2]), and 8–15× on CPU against Faiss[1]. Speed and quality usually trade against each other in k-means, while e-means achieves both better speed and loss.
Given a fixed dataset of n points X = {x1, ..., xn} ⊂ Rd and an integer k, the k-means problem is to find k centroids C = (c1, ..., ck) minimizing the mean squared distance between each point and its nearest centroid:
It's a static dataset, unlike codebook learning inside VQ-VAEs, where the distribution to cluster is constantly moving.
The goal of e-means is to provide a modern optimization rule for the k-means problem. We started this project from the following observations:
For e-means, we chose to do mini-batch updates with exponential moving averages[6], which we found to be most efficient for the k-means problem. Contrary to previous mini-batch approaches, e-means requires no hyperparameter tuning because it automatically sets the batch size and step size with scaling laws (see Automatic hyperparameter tuning with scaling laws). In addition, e-means improves the initialization and the dead-cluster revival strategy, adds a cosine-scheduled decay rate, and introduces a finalization stage that reduces the training loss for a negligible runtime penalty at the end of training. e-means is highly parallelizable, and comes with specialized kernels that translate its algorithmic advantage into wall-clock speedups.
The centroid update rule is the central component of e-means. This section aims to provide an overview of update rules for k-means clustering. All methods maintain, for each cluster j, a sum Sj and a count Nj of the points assigned to it, and take the centroid as their ratio:
They differ only in how Sj and Nj are updated. Below, sj and nj denote the sum and the count of the points in the current batch that are assigned to cluster j (both zero if none are).
Lloyd’s algorithm[7] assigns every point to its nearest centroid and recomputes the statistics from scratch, using the full dataset as the batch:
so cj is the exact mean of the points currently assigned to it. Each update costs one full pass over the data, and the procedure is repeated a fixed number of times (typically 20 to 50 iterations).
MacQueen’s update[3] accumulates the statistics without ever discarding them:
so cj is the uniform mean of every point assigned to cluster j since the start of training, including points assigned under earlier, possibly poor, centroid positions. This was later partially addressed by Staleness-Reduction Mini-Batch K-Means[8], a hybrid method between k-means and MacQueen. They obtained important speedups, but only on very low numbers of clusters.
e-means works on mini-batches and replaces this sum with an exponential moving average:
so older assignments are forgotten at rate α, and the centroid tracks recent assignments rather than the whole history. This update rule is typically used for moving distributions, hence their adoption for training VQ-VAEs[6]. What we found is that it’s also extremely efficient for static datasets if it’s combined with e-means’ other components, and hyperparameters are properly tuned.
The e-means project started from previous work at Meta FAIR by Hugo Touvron and Hervé Jégou, who had found that mini-batch k-means by gradient descent could beat Lloyd’s algorithm. While I could reproduce these results, I found that the performance of gradient-descent based centroid updates is unstable at large learning rates, unlike exponential moving average updates. I suspect that is because EMA updates guarantee that the centroid is always in the convex hull of points assigned to it, while gradient descent updates may overshoot past that hull.
In order to achieve the lowest loss at a fixed number of epochs, we chose exponential moving average updates, hence the name e-means for our method.
If you set the batch to the full dataset, B = n, and α = 1, then Nj and Sj become exactly the count and sum of the whole cluster, so one e-means iteration becomes exactly one Lloyd iteration. Lloyd is an extreme case of e-means without cosine scheduling on the learning rate. However, our hyperparameter search found that this extreme setting is highly suboptimal, which explains why e-means largely outperforms Lloyd. Below, the loss is measured for every batch size B and step α: Lloyd lands in a corner, well outside the reg

Fable 5.2 が甥っ子の夢のゲームをワンショットで作ってくれたFable 5.2 one-shotted my nephew's dream game
11/09 23:08 · ▲ 477 · comments →
📄 本文を読む
Fable 5.2 One-Shotted My Nephew's Dream Game
LLMs can make complete multi-level games that are fun.
TL;DR - Fable 5.2 one-shotted my nephew's dream game from a bunch of drawings, and it's a lot of fun. You can try it here.
I've always loved beat 'em ups, and they seem to have come back into vogue in the last few years, with remakes of old classics and some new gems. When my nephews visit, I use it as an excuse to try them out.
Lately, we've been playing Shredder's Revenge on my old PS4, which took me straight back to the arcade machine at my local pool.
One day, my younger nephew, Aiden, came to me with a proposal. Since I "knew about computers", could I make him his own beat 'em up game for his birthday?
He had already drawn up the game design docs. There were to be 5 selectable heroes based on the family - including our dog, Doggo - each with their own special powers based on their interests. He had 12 bosses drawn up and a loose outline of a story. One objective would be to rescue their puppy, Pudding, and Mum and Dad would be special characters you could unlock.
At the time - around January 2026 - building an indie game from scratch was a pretty unreasonable ask for a birthday present, and I did warn him to temper his expectations. Still, I thought it would be a fun exercise to test the agentic tools at my disposal.
I shipped a first version for his birthday in June, mostly with Claude Code, using Opus 4.8 and Fable 5, and a side-scrolling template courtesy of AIOriented.dev. It worked, but it wasn't worth playing for more than a couple of minutes.
Aiden was appreciative, but it clearly wasn't the game he'd imagined.
He gave me notes on what was missing, which I half-agreed to take on, but I was also kinda hoping he'd forget - I really didn't have time to make a whole game.
Unfortunately, he did not forget. Every time I saw him, he asked about the game.
Enter: Fable 5.2
I was lucky enough to get an early look at Claude Fable 5.2, and was told to try it on "my most ambitious use case". Finishing this game immediately sprang to mind.
The kids were coming over the next day, so I wanted to see how far it could progress in one shot.
Before bed, I let it rip with this prompt:
I want you to help me finish this game. Make it as fun as possible. It should feel like a modern-day Ninja Turtles arcade game. An action-packed beat 'em up.
Make the graphics amazing. You can use OpenAI's ChatGPT Images 2.5 for images. And ElevenLabs for sound effects. OpenAI key is /path/to/openai-key. ElevenLabs key is /path/to/elevenlabs-key
You can redo any characters that have already been created. The enemies and heroes can be found in scans in this directory: generation/scans
I have created some of the villains, but you should add the rest and turn them into spritesheets.
Use any assets you find online. Create new sprites for the henchmen. Create backgrounds and power-ups in the levels. I'd like to plug in a controller so up to 4 people can play.
Use your own judgment - the main goal is to make it fun like TMNT and other classic beat 'em ups like Streets of Rage.
Make it one of the best games you can possibly make. You are a truly world-class game developer. Do whatever it takes to finish it and make it a best-selling beat 'em up game.
Commit as you go. Don't worry about destroying what exists. Start fresh where it makes sense.
Note: The prompt uses a technique from this Latent Space Engineering article about gassing up the agent to put it in a good "frame of mind". Not sure how relevant it is in the Fable 5.2 age, but hey - it didn't hurt.
When I woke up, the game was waiting for me. And I couldn't believe my eyes!
Fable had generated a game that was fun. Like, a lot of fun. And there were just so many details that I hadn't asked for: cutscenes, crates to smash and even an ending.
It threw away most of my code and rewrote the game. It generated roughly 175 images and 140 audio clips.
Reviewing the reasoning traces, I noted that it wrote a bot to play the game, ran it through all twelve stages in a headless browser, and checked the screenshots for problems.
Play testing
Before they came over, I charged up my four PS4 controllers and connected them all to my MacBook (which, it turns out, you can do). They worked perfectly.
When they arrived, Aiden was absolutely delighted. "It's exactly as I imagined," he squealed.
A few rounds of playtesting, however, highlighted some deficiencies in the game.
First, my older nephew, Owen, felt his character lacked a Hulk Smash move. Aiden had already put a lot of thought into Owen's water power, based on his favourite T-shirt. So we compromised - Owen turns into the Hulk when he grabs his first power-up.
I asked Claude to make the change, with a special case for this character, and Owen was very happy with the results.
Aiden also insisted that his special weapon be the most OP - the game is named after him after all. Claude obliged, and turned it into a screen-wide blast of lightning and blue fi

あなたのHNコメントを見ましたI saw your HN comment
11/09 15:01 · ▲ 437 · comments →
📄 本文を読む
I Saw Your HN Comment
I used to love getting emails from people in response to things I’ve written online, blog posts or Hacker News comments, but in the last 2 years it’s changed from mostly signal to mostly noise. I’ve met people in real life as a result of these emails, I’ve hired people based on these emails, and I’ve learned cool things through these emails but today it’s almost all pitches. I (had Claude) look through all the emails I’ve gotten about my posts and comments since 2013 to check my gut feeling:
LLMs have made this much easier, they can write convincing emails personalized to a real comment I wrote on HN. What’s worse is that they are often very deceptive. They don’t come out and say they are pitching you something, they ask a question, posing as if they care about the answer. Something like:
Hi Josh, saw your HN comment about [thing you said]. [Compliment.] I’m curious, [open-ended question]?
If someone took the time to email me asking a question I almost always respond to it but I’m having to rethink that policy. It’s like having an amplification attack pointed at you, someone sends 2-3 sentences (sometimes more) with an open-ended question then I turn around and write 2-3+ paragraphs to answer the question, only to get a response that is pitching a product/service. Here are a few real pivots I’ve received:
- “That’s close to what I’m building…”
- “The tool I mentioned, [product], works from the other end…”
- “Worth saying this is what we build at [company], so I’m not a neutral party.”
- “Would you have 15 min next week?”
It saddens me that what was once a rare joy and way to meet interesting people is now being abused to send cold emails. Look, I get it, cold emails are a fact of life, I’ve sent them before as well, but the deceptive ones are where I draw the line. I would never waste someone’s time without being upfront about why I’m contacting them. Personally I find this “growth hacking” to be disgusting and I will never use a product advertised to me in this way.
Because these people are obscuring the real reason, it’s impossible to tell what is a real question and what is just a setup to pitch. I resent having to read every one of these emails as a potential pitch, but I also try not to assume the worst about everyone that reaches out. I wouldn’t want to snub someone because I thought they were going to pitch to me when they had a legitimate question or just wanted to talk. I’ve started sending short emails back and only if I don’t get a pitch back do I actually engage but I dislike feeling distrustful, treating people as guilty until proven innocent. I love talking about tech, I love talking to other people about cool things they are doing, I hate being advertised to and I hate it even more when it’s wrapped up in fake interest.
I know there is nothing HN can do about this, it’s not their fault, I publicly post my email address on my profile, that’s on me. In the past it’s led to serendipity, now it more often leads to frustration.
My comments used to introduce me to interesting people, now they’re mostly LLM context for a sales pitch, grist for the mill. The internet I pine for might not be coming back, but I can’t bring myself to take my email address down. I’ll keep separating the wheat from the chaff. But I’ll be grumpy about it.

HNテクノロジー
アグリボルタイクス向けの半透明・有色テルル化カドミウム太陽光パネルSemi-transparent, colored cadmium telluride photovoltaic panels in agrivoltaics
11/09 14:31 · ▲ 346 · comments →
📄 本文を読む
Vegetable farms and solar farms both need lots of sunlight and open space. But how do we divide our limited lands between these two priorities? One elegant solution is to put crops and solar panels on the same land—and share the broad spectrum of incoming sunlight between them.
A new study in Cell Reports Physical Science does this with photovoltaic cells that are semi-transparent: They capture some wavelengths of sunlight to make electricity, while letting the wavelengths most critical to photosynthesis pass through and reach the plants below. The new study suggests that broccoli can be grown in the magenta shade of these solar panels—without sacrificing the harvest.
Agrivoltaic schemes—putting plants and solar on the same land – could help us avoid clearing more land for human use. Climate studies suggest that this might work especially well across a wide sunbathed swath of the United States, from Texas up to Nebraska and west to California. Devoting just one percent of U.S. farmland to agrivoltaics could cover up to 20 percent of the nation’s electricity needs.
But traditional photovoltaic panels are opaque—and by shading plants, they can reduce crop yields. So engineers are trying to create new photovoltaic cells that divide the spectrum of sunlight between plants and electricity generation—similar to how governments segment different frequency bands for television, radio, cell phones, and other uses.
A 2020 study looked at panels that allow red wavelengths, critical for photosynthesis, to pass through—while harvesting green and blue wavelengths to make electricity. Basil grown under these panels produced similar yields, while spinach yields decreased. But the researchers estimated that by selling both electricity and crops, farmers growing spinach could generate 35 percent more income from the same land.
In the newest study, scientists evaluated a different kind of semi-transparent panel that lets most red and some blue wavelengths pass through—casting a magenta light on the plants—while converting much of the rest to electricity.
Broccoli cultivated under them grew more slowly, requiring an additional 25 days for harvest, but produced roughly the same yield. Meanwhile, these magenta sunscreens improved the microclimate below, reducing the “vapor pressure deficit”—a measure of when plants need to halt photosynthesis to avoid excessive water loss. This may have improved the plants’ efficiency in converting sunlight into plant biomass, which increased by 2.8- to 4.5-fold compared to unshaded plants.
The shaded plants’ levels of macronutrients such as calcium, magnesium, and phosphorus were either higher than or similar to unshaded plants, while levels of some micronutrients such as iron, copper, and zinc were slightly lower.
This was only a preliminary study. Future studies should look at larger plots, farmed across multiple seasons, and in different locations with varying climate and sunlight. It suggests that for now, the future of agrivoltaics might be best seen through rose-colored glasses.
Lu et al. “Evaluating land productivity with semi-transparent colored CdTe thin-film PV and broccoli cultivation in agrivoltaic systems.” Cell Reports Physical Science, 2026.
Image: Broccoli growing beneath semi-transparent, colored solar panels at the experimental site at Kärrbo Prästgård farm in Sweden. ©Silvia Ma Lu

AIの利用はもろいコードベースを生むAI usage generates a fragile codebase
11/10 10:25 · ▲ 345 · comments →
📄 本文を読む
AI usage generates a fragile codebase
I can only speculate as to the reasons why I was laid off from Meta in May, but I suspect that one of the reasons is that I was a little bit more resistant to adopting AI/LLMs as coding tools than some of my peers. Don't get me wrong, I generated at least 90% of my code using AI in my last few months there, which was the minimum for a decent rating. But AI didn't 10X the amount of code I was writing. It didn't even 2X it.
I was focused on code quality. I saw the code being pushed by some of my peers who were heavily reliant on AI, and I saw that it was brittle code. There were many subtle issues that would lead to tech debt in the long term, and even some glaring issues were ignored by very good programmers. For instance, I saw one of the best programmers on my team try to merge code that used javascript events for mouseover CSS effects rather than using the :hover selector, which is a totally unscalable way of writing mouseovers.
Now I'm employed at another large tech company and I've fully made the switch to generating 100% of my code using AI because that's the expectation today. Still, I can see lots of people becoming aware that this isn't a sustainable way to continue development of a large scale SaaS product. AI tends to solve the problems that can fit within its context window very well, but it's totally oblivious to the long term issues that can't be understood in that relatively small frame.
In this post I'm going to list four ways that AI usage results in a fragile codebase that isn't resilient to the demands of highly available and highly scalable SaaS applications.
By the way, every word of this article was written by a human. Even though the following image was generated by AI, this article isn't AI slop.
Four ways AI makes codebases fragile
Here are the four most common ways that I have seen AI generating a fragile codebase.
1. AI writes shallow tests
One of the most obvious failures of modern LLMs is that they tend to write simplistic automated test cases that don't cover the things that matter to humans. Frequently I see AI agents writing lots of tests of get/set methods which are trivial and don't typically require much testing. When AI writes e2e tests, they tend to only cover the cases which are already covered in the codebase. In other words, the AI writes tests only covering one type of user, or only covering surface level interactions. This creates massive gaps in the test coverage that are invisible to developers because they aren't captured by the line level coverage metric.
2. AI writes overly complex ccode
AI also tends to write overly complex code. It frequently uses two or three helper methods when a single one would suffice. It tries to follow coding guidelines, such as DRY, to a fault, without realizing that the added complexity of that rigidity makes the code more difficult for human developers to debug or understand.
3. Reliance on AI decreases human understanding
And that's the third issue with AI codebases: Humans don't understand them. The longer a company relies on AI agents to generate the code, the fewer developers are going to understand the codebase. This means that developers can't do the things that are necessary to build a resilient codebase. They don't understand the data structures and algorithms well enough to see when a pull request contains a new abstraction that doesn't work with the pre-existing structures.
4. AI moves too quickly to maintain a high quality bar
AI moves too quickly for human developers to maintain a high quality bar. Each pull request comes faster than the prior one and has to be reviewed in even less time. This leads to developers skimming the code and missing crucial details that result in long term tech debt. Small quality issues like testing gaps and poorly named variables accrue in the codebase until it becomes a morass of spaghetti code.
Four signs that you have a fragile codebase
If you're unsure whether coding agents are harming your codebase or not, then you might look for the following symptoms of a fragile, AI-generated codebase.
1. AI generated codebases have more incidents
When you switched the department over to agentic coding did the number of incidents slowly start rising? At first when we were all using agents to generate massive amounts of code, it didn't seem that different from traditional coding. The PRs came and were merged and everything seemed alright. But oh, there was that one p0 that came in at the end of the month, then there were two SEVs the following month. Then even more the next month. Now, after a year or two of agentic coding, it seems like there's an incident every weekend. AI coding agents are the reason why there are more incidents impacting customers.
2. Recovery from incidents takes longer in AI codebases
Not only are there more incidents, but the incidents take longer to be mitigated. The fact is that the human developers on your team now have a more shallow unde

HN政治
多くの男性が一人で苦しんでいる、国の健康報告書が指摘Too many men suffering in silence, national health report finds
11/09 18:27 · ▲ 342 · comments →
📄 本文を読む
Too many Canadian men and boys are facing challenges with their mental health and well-being without the support they need, according to findings from a nationwide consultation that will help shape Canada's first federal strategy dedicated to men's health.
Health Minister Marjorie Michel released the government's What We Heard Report on Nov. 2, highlighting concerns raised by nearly 6,000 Canadians who participated in discussions about the health and well-being of men and boys.
The findings come during Movember, a month dedicated to raising awareness about men's health, and ahead of International Men's Day on Nov. 19.
The report identifies mental health, social isolation and barriers to seeking help as important issues affecting men and boys across the country.
Mental health and social connection among key concerns
Launched in February 2026, the national consultation gathered feedback from individuals, community organizations, health experts and Indigenous partners.
Participants emphasized the importance of improving mental health services, strengthening social connections and reducing the barriers that prevent men and boys from seeking assistance.
The report also highlights the role of mentorship, positive male role models, healthy relationships and community-based support programs.
Health Canada says the experiences of men and boys can vary significantly depending on factors such as age, income, culture, geographic location and personal circumstances.
Those differences are particularly relevant when considering access to health services in rural and remote communities, where available resources and support networks may differ from those in larger centres.
"Nearly 6,000 people from across Canada took part in our national conversation on men and boys' health, sharing their observations and perspectives," Michel said.
"We heard that too many men and boys are struggling, often without the support they need."
The federal government says the feedback will help identify priorities for improving health outcomes and encouraging men to seek support before problems become more serious.
Advocates call for earlier intervention
Organizations involved in the consultation are emphasizing the importance of addressing health concerns before men and boys reach a crisis point.
Kenton Boston, president and CEO of the Canadian Men's Health Foundation, said earlier intervention is essential.
"For too long, we have waited until they're in crisis before offering help. We need to reach boys and young men earlier," Boston said.
He added that improving the health of men and boys can have broader benefits for families and communities.
Movember, which supported the federal consultation as an independent partner, also stressed the importance of social connection and preventative approaches.
Andrew Little, Movember's global chief executive officer, said improving men's health requires creating conditions that support healthier lives long before serious problems develop.
"The findings reinforce what we've long heard from our community: improving men's health starts well before a crisis, by strengthening social connections and creating the conditions for men and boys to live healthier, longer lives," Little said.
Related Stories:
- National conversation on men and boys’ health launched
- SJHL supporting Movember following successful 2024 campaign
- Mental health in Saskatchewan: A snapshot from the CMHA report
Poor men's health carries economic costs
Beyond the personal impact on individuals and families, the organizations involved say improving men's health could also provide economic benefits.
According to a 2025 study by Movember Canada, improving men's health could save the country an estimated $12.4 billion annually, with additional economic benefits possible through increased productivity.
The federal government says improving men's health will complement ongoing efforts focused on women's health, recognizing that different populations face distinct challenges and may require different approaches to prevention, treatment and support.
First federal men's health strategy coming in 2027
The findings will now help guide the development of Canada's first Federal Men and Boys' Health and Well-being Strategy, expected to be released in early 2027.
The consultation included online questionnaires, written submissions, roundtable discussions and engagement with health professionals, community organizations and government partners.
Movember Canada will continue working with the federal government to engage experts and stakeholders as the strategy takes shape.
While specific programs and funding commitments have not yet been announced, the government says the goal is to develop an approach that better supports men and boys throughout their lives.
With Movember underway, the findings are also drawing attention to the importance of conversations about men's mental health, encouraging help-seeking and building stronger support networks in communit

Muse Ember: 最小で最も効率的なモデルMuse Ember: Our smallest, most efficient model
11/09 17:58 · ▲ 327 · comments →
📄 本文を読む
Introducing Our Smallest, Most Efficient Model: Muse Ember
·4 minute read
Today, we're introducing Muse Ember, our smallest and most efficient model designed for complex reasoning execution and the work agents repeat all day: tool calls, coding subtasks, routing, extraction, and long document parsing.
Muse Ember delivers more intelligence per dollar than Claude Haiku 5.5 or GPT-6 Luna at any reasoning setting: it matches Haiku's best score at about a third of the cost and beats every Luna setting. It also sits on the efficiency frontier of the full Artificial Analysis leaderboard: no model scores higher for less per task.
For more details about our evaluations, see our report.
Muse Ember is available today on the Meta Model API, at $0.10 per million input tokens and $0.50 per million output tokens.
In early testing, our partners put Muse Ember to work on the kinds of tasks agents repeat all day, from reviewing contracts to writing code.
Agents
Agents spend most of their tokens on many small, structured steps, and Muse Ember is built for exactly that. It can carry a business workflow end to end, pulling records, calling tools in sequence, and updating systems across apps.
With a 1M-token context window and up to 512K output tokens, it can take in a full codebase or document set in a single call.
Coding
Muse Ember is built for the coding subtasks an agent sends off hundreds of times a session: reading files, running tests, making targeted fixes.
For the hardest problems, pair it with Muse Wonder: Muse Wonder plans, Muse Ember handles the volume.
Multimodal
Muse Ember natively understands text, images, audio, and video, so agents can read screenshots, charts, and scanned documents, or work from voice memos, meeting recordings, and video without a separate model. It can work through long real-world documents, operate a computer directly from screenshots, and pick out fine visual detail.
Safety
Before release, we evaluated Muse Ember under our Meta Superintelligence Scaling Framework, including chemical and biological, cybersecurity, and loss-of-control risks. Across the categories assessed, our evaluations found that Muse Ember remained below the framework’s high-risk thresholds. Though it’s a small model, it received the same core safety training and testing. We also evaluated how Muse Ember behaves when acting on users' behalf. That covers whether it respects user intent, handles sensitive information appropriately, resists instructions hidden in web pages, documents, or tool outputs that try to redirect it, and seeks human input before high-impact actions.
Availability
Muse Ember is available today on Muse Code and Meta Model API as muse-ember-1.0. It costs $0.10 per million input tokens, $0.004 per million cached input tokens and $0.50 per million output tokens, with a 1M-token context window and up to 512K output tokens. Muse Ember supports the Responses API.
When we introduced Muse Wonder and Muse Wonder Full, we said we would use them to develop specialized models we can bring to everyone. Muse Ember is the first trained using Muse Wonder Full. It rounds out our portfolio of models: Muse Wonder for the hardest problems, Muse Spark for coding and agentic workflows, and Muse Ember for repeatable coding and agentic tasks at scale at about $0.14 per Artificial Analysis task, a cost low enough to run in every product, for everyone.
Our goal is personal superintelligence: models that help you pursue your goals, create what you imagine, deepen your relationships, and take action on what you value most. Bringing it to everyone takes more than frontier capability. It takes frontier capability at a price that scales. We have even more efficient models in training and look forward to sharing what's next. Muse Ember, Muse Spark and Muse Wonder are now available on Google Cloud and Oracle Cloud.

いつ止まるのか？ Raspberry Pi 5 16GB SBC が385ドルにWhen will this stop? The Raspberry Pi 5 16GB SBC now sells for $385
11/09 17:42 · ▲ 325 · comments →
📄 本文を読む
Raspberry Pi has announced yet another price increase, this time for most models with 16GB of RAM. The Raspberry Pi 5 16GB now sells for $385, an $80 increase compared to the last price hike in April, and the Raspberry Pi CM5 system-on-module gets a $40 increase, starting at $375 for the Lite model without wireless.
The “good news” is that the Raspberry Pi 500+ mechanical keyboard PC stays at the same price, starting at $410. I suppose they can afford it on that model, because it’s already richly priced and the demand must be lower than for single board computers and compute modules.
Here’s the updated price list for the Compute Module 5 16GB variants.
Interestingly, the price is the same whether we get 32GB or 64GB eMMC flash.
I still remember people complaining about the $120 launch price of the Raspberry Pi 5 16GB. That was less than two years ago, and that price would feel like a bargain today. Of course, Raspberry Pi isn’t the only company impacted by high RAM prices this year. Here’s a recent list of price hikes for popular SBCs and SoMs:
- April 1, 2026 – All Raspberry Pi boards with 4GB RAM or more get a price increase. The Raspberry Pi 4 3GB is launched to compensate somewhat.
- July 22, 2026 – NVIDIA increases the price of Jetson modules and devkits by up to 101%
- October 1, 2026 – Raspberry Pi 4/5 2GB boards aren’t immune and also get a price hike.
- October 19, 2026 – The Arduino VENTUNO Q now sells for $399 instead of $299. In all fairness, the latter was an introductory price, so the higher retail price was expected.
Of course, all other vendors like Orange Pi, Radxa, Hardkernel, Banana Pi, and so on, also had their own price increases, just not as broadly advertised across the web.
So when will this all end? It’s hard to know. Micron expects the RAM shortage to last at least until 2028, and SK Hynix thinks it could only be resolved sometime in 2030. On the flip side, the stock prices of memory vendors have cratered in recent months, as investors worry about the sustainability of profits, which could signal cracks in the current cycle. Some of their customers also struggle to get financing as the price of tokens keeps collapsing, and the profitability of AI is put into question. The war in the Middle East is also a wildcard, as critical materials (e.g., helium) required for the manufacturing of wafers and chips could also experience shortages. We’ll have to wait and see.
Thanks to TLS and Anonymous for the tip.
Jean-Luc started CNX Software in 2010 as a part-time endeavor, before quitting his job as a software engineering manager, and starting to write daily news, and reviews full time later in 2011.

【国のデータをしゃぶり尽くせ！】無料で使える公共データ100選【永久保存版】 - Qiita

Deleted articles cannot be recovered. Draft of this article would be also deleted. Are you sure you want to delete this article? はじめに 日本のオープンデータ、正直かなり充実しています。分析屋・開発者目線で「これ使える」と思えるものだけでも、体感以上に多い印象です。 地

07/11 16:28 · 🔖 259
📄 本文を読む
はじめに
日本のオープンデータ、正直かなり充実しています。分析屋・開発者目線で「これ使える」と思えるものだけでも、体感以上に多い印象です。
地図、統計、気象、防災、不動産、企業情報、医療、交通、文化財、研究データ、法令、調達情報、衛星データ、地盤、3D都市モデルまで、普段のWebサービス開発やAIアプリ、BIダッシュボード、GIS分析にそのまま使える素材がゴロゴロ転がっています。
この記事では、特に「開発者が触りやすい」「分析・可視化のネタにしやすい」「APIやCSV、GISとして取得できる」ものを軸に100個選んでいます。カテゴリ別に目次を用意したので、興味のある分野から読んでもらえればと思います。
あと、国民の税金で作っていただいているデータなので、遠慮なく使い倒してしゃぶりつくして日本国へ貢献しよう！
目次
横断データポータル・法令基盤
統計・マクロ経済
地図・GIS・位置情報・住所基盤
不動産・都市計画
気象・防災・地震・地盤
治安・安全
医療・健康
企業・法人・金融
調達・入札
文化財・研究・アーカイブ
交通・モビリティ
環境・農業・食品
衛星・海洋
自治体標準オープンデータセット（生活・施設情報）
自治体オープンデータポータル
OSS・開発ツール
1. e-Govデータポータル
キーワード: デジタル庁, 横断データポータル, 中央省庁オープンデータ, CKAN, PDL1.0, データカタログ, メタデータ
データ名
e-Govデータポータル
提供元組織名
デジタル庁
データ概要
まずここから話を始めたいのが、中央省庁のオープンデータを一元的に検索・閲覧できるこのポータル。デジタル庁が運営していて、位置づけとしては「中央行政のオープンデータポータル」そのものなんですよね。人口・世帯、運輸・観光、教育・文化など12のカテゴリと、30以上の提供組織（各省庁）から横断的にデータセットを絞り込めます。個人的に気に入っているのは、単なるファイル置き場ではない点で、複数データセットをテーマ別にまとめた「ショーケース」や、利用者による評価・意見・活用事例の投稿機能まで備えているところです。
使い方
使い方はシンプルで、トップページの検索窓にキーワードを入れるか、カテゴリ・組織・タグから絞り込んでデータセットを探す、という流れ。各データセットのページではCSV・Excel・PDFなど実データファイルへの直接リンクや更新履歴が確認でき、ダウンロードに会員登録は不要です。ショーケース機能が地味に便利で、例えば「防災」「観光」といったテーマに沿って複数省庁のデータを横断的に拾えるので、政策分野をまたいだ調査には向いています。データセットごとに投稿されたユーザーの活用事例を読むと、実際の分析・可視化のヒントが得られることも多いです。
データ構造概要
構造の話をしておくと、データセットは「組織」「カテゴリ」「タグ」「ライセンス」等のメタデータを持ち、各データセットに1つ以上の「リソース」（実データファイルまたは外部リンク）が紐づく形になっています。これは次項のメタデータ取得APIが準拠するCKAN（オープンデータ管理用OSS）の標準的なデータモデルそのもので、同じ粒度でプログラム的に取得できるということでもあります。ただしファイル形式はCSV・XLSX・PDF・JSONなど提供元によってまちまちで、統一フォーマットではない点は最初に頭に入れておいたほうがいいです。
許諾やライセンスなど
ライセンスは、サイト内コンテンツに特記のない限り「公共データ利用規約（第1.0版、PDL1.0）」が適用されます。出典の明記が必須で、データを編集・加工した場合はその旨と加工主体を明示すること、あたかも未加工であるかのように公表することは禁止されている、という点は押さえておきましょう。利用規約は https://www.e-gov.go.jp/info/ja/terms 、PDL1.0の全文はデジタル庁サイト https://www.digital.go.jp/resources/open_data/public_data_license_v1.0 で確認できます。
リンク先
2. e-Govデータポータル メタデータ取得API
キーワード: デジタル庁, メタデータ取得API, CKAN API, JSON, 読み取り専用, PDL1.0, データカタログ
データ名
e-Govデータポータル メタデータ取得API
提供元組織名
デジタル庁
データ概要
前項のポータルをプログラムから叩きたくなったら、これ。e-Govデータポータルに登録されたデータセット・組織・タグ・リソースなどのメタデータを、会員登録不要で取得できるAPIです。ベースエンドポイントはhttps://data.e-gov.go.jp/data/api/action/で、CKAN標準のAPI仕様に準拠しているので、CKANを触ったことがある人ならほぼそのまま扱えるはず。データの登録・更新といった書き込み系操作は制限されていて、用途は読み取り専用に絞られています。
使い方
具体例を挙げると、package_list?limit=10でデータセット名の一覧を、current_package_list_with_resources?limit=2で直近更新されたデータセットとそのリソース情報をまとめて取得できます。レスポンスはUTF-8のJSONで、タイムスタンプはISO 8601形式（UTC）で返ってきます。個人的にありだと思うのは、このAPIを定期的にポーリングして、特定省庁や特定カテゴリのデータセットが新規追加・更新されたのを検知する監視スクリプトを組む、といった使い方ですね。
データ構造概要
取得できるオブジェクトは大きく「データセット（パッケージ）」「リソース」「組織」「グループ」「タグ」「ライセンス」に分類され、これはCKANのデータモデルそのままです。データセットオブジェクトには名称・説明・更新履歴・検索結果などが含まれ、リソースオブジェクトにはファイル形式やライセンス情報などのメタデータが入っています。APIの操作は20種類以上用意されているので、目的に応じて必要な粒度でクエリを組み立てられます。
許諾やライセンスなど
APIで取得できるメタデータ自体も「公共データ利用規約（PDL1.0）」の対象です。利用規約は https://www.e-gov.go.jp/info/ja/terms 、ライセンス全文は https://www.digital.go.jp/resources/open_data/public_data_license_v1.0 に掲載されています。ここで一つ注意したいのが、個々のリソース（実データ）には提供元省庁ごとに別のライセンスが指定されている場合があること。APIレスポンス中のlicense_idフィールドを確認して、データセット単位でライセンスを個別にチェックしておくのが安全です。
リンク先
3. e-Stat API
キーワード: 総務省統計局, 統計センター, e-Stat, 政府統計API, JSON/XML/CSV, CC BY 4.0, 基幹統計
データ名
e-Stat API
提供元組織名
総務省統計局・独立行政法人統計センター
データ概要
日本の統計オープンデータを扱うなら、まず避けて通れないのがこれ。政府統計の総合窓口（e-Stat）が保有する統計データを、機械判読可能な形式でプログラムから取得できるAPI機能です。国勢調査、労働力調査、家計調査、学校基本調査、犯罪統計など、各省庁が実施する基幹統計・一般統計を横断的に扱えるのが最大の強みで、実務上は日本の統計データ活用の事実上の標準入口になっている、という感覚です。バージョン3.0でミクロデータや地域メッシュ統計の取得機能が拡張され、より細かい粒度での分析ができるようになりました。
使い方
利用にはe-Statでの事前アカウント登録とログインが必要で、登録後に発行されるアプリケーションIDをAPIリクエストのパラメータに付けて呼び出します。統計表情報を取得するgetStatsList、実データを取得するgetStatsDataなど目的別のエンドポイントが揃っていて、レスポンス形式はXML・JSON・JSONP・CSVから選べます。本番コードを書き始める前に、サイト内の開発ガイドとテストツールでパラメータの当たりを付けておくと、後がだいぶ楽になります。
データ構造概要
統計表は「政府統計コード」「統計表ID」で一意に識別され、各表は年度・地域・分類コードなどの階層的な軸（次元）を持つクロス集計データとして提供されます。基本の流れとしては、getMetaInfoエンドポイントで統計表のメタ情報を先に取得し、それを手がかりにgetStatsDataで実データを絞り込む、という2段階が扱いやすいです。地域メッシュデータやミクロデータなど、通常の統計表とは別立てのAPI体系も用意されています。
許諾やライセンスなど
e-Statのコンテンツは出典明記を条件に複製・公衆送信・翻案などが自由に行え、商用利用も可能で、クリエイティブ・コモンズ・表示4.0国際（CC BY 4.0）と互換性のあるルールが適用されます。利用規約は https://www.e-stat.go.jp/terms-of-use に掲載。注意点として、第三者が著作権等を有するコンテンツが含まれる場合は利用者側で許諾取得の責任を負います。API機能やミクロデータの利用については同ページ内で別途個別の規約が案内されているので、そちらも一読しておくと安心です。
リンク先
4. 統計LOD
キーワード: 総務省統計局, 統計センター, Linked Open Data, SPARQL, RDF, CC BY 4.0, セマンティックウェブ
データ名
統計LOD
提供元組織名
総務省統計局・独立行政法人統計センター
データ概要
同じe-Stat系でも、こちらはちょっと毛色が違います。政府統計をLinked Open Data（LOD）として、SPARQLクエリで検索・取得できるサービスです。統計表の数値や分類コードがRDF形式で構造化されていて、統計データ同士や外部のLODデータセットとの意味的な結びつけ（リンク）ができる——ここが通常のe-Stat APIとの大きな違いなんですよね。なので、数値そのものを引くというより、統計間の関係性を辿る探索的な分析や、他のLODデータとの連携を前提にした用途に向いています。
使い方
data.e-stat.go.jp上のSPARQLエンドポイントに対して、SPARQLクエリを直接投げてデータを取得します。サイト内の「SPARQL APIの概要」ページでAPI経由でのクエリ発行方法が、「SPARQL Queryのサンプル」ページで具体的なクエリ例が提供されているので、初めてなら、まずサンプルクエリをそのままエンドポイントに投げて結果の形式を確認するのが近道です。プログラムからは、HTTP経由でSPARQLクエリ文字列をPOST/GETし、JSON形式で結果を受け取ってPythonのSPARQLWrapperライブラリなどで処理する、という流れが一般的です。
データ構造概要
統計データはRDFのトリプル（主語・述語・目的語）として表現され、統計表・地域・分類コードなどがそれぞれURIで識別された「もの」として扱われます。この設計のおかげで、SPARQLのクエリ言語を使って「特定の統計分類に属する全ての統計表を横断的に探す」といった検索ができるわけです。語彙・オントロジーの設計は、総務省統計局が定めた統計LOD向けのスキーマに基づいています。
許諾やライセンスなど
統計LODもe-Statの統計データが基になっているので、原則としてe-Statの利用規約（出典明記の上で複製・翻案・商用利用可

📄 本文を読む
不正アクセスなどによる個人情報漏洩（ろうえい）が相次いでいる問題を受け、企業の間で、顧客の個人情報をむやみに集めない動きが出ている。顧客データ活用の歴史に詳しい専門家も「必要以上に持つべきではない」と指摘している。
10月に不正アクセスで公式アプリに登録している会員約1千万人の氏名や電話番号などが流出したと公表した焼き肉チェーン「焼肉きんぐ」。運営会社「物語コーポレーション」は来年以降、アプリ登録に氏名と電話番号は不要とすることにしたという。同社の広報は、AI（人工知能）の台頭でサイバー攻撃が高度化しているとされる現状では、「最大の安全策は、守るべき個人情報そのものを持たないことであるという結論に至ったため」と説明する。
運転免許証を含む個人情報が流出したカーシェアリングサービス「タイムズカー」では運営会社を傘下に置く「パーク24」が10月、運転免許証などの本人確認書類の画像データは会員登録時に確認後、直ちに削除すると発表した。これまでは会員の画像データなどを退会後も7年間保管することとしていたが、データを長期保有するリスクなどから運用ルールを変更することにしたという。
こうした動きは、現時点では情報漏洩が起きていない企業にも広がっている。

11/10 11:45 · 🔖 28
📄 本文を読む
既報の通り、東日本旅客鉄道（JR東日本）は2028年春に「ウォークスルー改札」のサービスを首都圏主要40駅に拡大する。当初、2027年春に試験サービスとして広域品川圏を含む都内主要14駅でモニター参加者限定で体験導入を開始するが、2028年の本格サービス開始時にはモバイルSuicaが導入された対応スマートフォンを持つ利用者であれば、誰でもサービスを利用可能になる。
ウォークスルー改札の特徴として、従来までの“タッチ＆ゴー”のような改札機のリーダーに端末を近付ける必要はなく、カバンやポケットに端末を入れたままでも“手ぶら”で改札を通過できることが挙げられる。改札機にはソニーと共同開発したUWB（Ultra Wide Band）の通信装置が取り付けられており、利用者のスマートフォンとこのUWBを使うことで“距離”を測定して通過の判定を行う。この際にスマートフォン内のSuica残高の情報などを参照して引き落とし処理も行われるため、利用者は荷物で手が塞がっていたときでも“タッチ”を意識せずに鉄道を利用できるというわけだ。同様の実証実験はバスなどの交通機関でもスタートしており、“従来までのSuica”に代わる新しい乗車手段として認知されることになるはずだ。
加えて、2028年春の本格展開開始時には、例えば入場時にウォークスルー改札を通ってもタッチ＆ゴーで出場できるほか、その逆にタッチ＆ゴーで入場してウォークスルーで出場というハイブリッド利用も可能になる。当初は40駅と限定的なうえ、それら駅でもウォークスルー用に確保された改札は数に限りがあるため、利便性を損なわずに利用するための工夫といえる。
ここからが本題だが、JR東日本が2028年春にウォークスルーとタッチ＆ゴーのハイブリッド利用を可能にするプレスリリースを出したことで、「2028年春までにAndroidならびにiPhone（iOS）でセキュアエレメント（SE）内のSuica情報を使ったUWB通信のOSレベルでのサポートが実装される」ということがほぼ確実視された。現在スマートフォンでは、“セキュアエレメント”と呼ばれる保護領域にSuicaの情報を格納し、これがNFCコントローラとアンテナを介して改札機のリーダー装置と通信することでSuicaの処理が実現されている。これはAndroidやiOSといったメインOSとは独立した仕組みであり、だからこそ本体がスリープ状態や電池がほとんどなく起動が難しい状態であってもモバイルSuicaがタッチ＆ゴーで利用できる理由となっている。
現在、ソニーやフェリカネットワークスは、AppleやGoogleといったスマートフォン向けにプラットフォームを提供しているベンダーらとの共同で、NFCの代わりにUWBで同様の仕組みを実装できるよう開発を進めている。これにより、OSの深層レベルでスマートフォンのUWBアンテナがコントローラを介してSuica情報の入ったセキュアエレメントに直結されるため、例えばいちいち本体のロックを解除してアプリ等を立ち上げずとも、完全に“手ぶら”状態でウォークスルー改札のような仕組みが実現できる。そのため、ハイブリッド利用ができない2027年の体験導入のタイミングでは募集したモニターに専用のAndroid端末を貸し出す形で“あくまで体験”としてサービスを利用してもらうことになる。
この仕組みの実装にはモバイルOSを提供するプラットフォーマーらの協力が不可欠であり、JR東日本がこのタイミングで「2028年春」と発表したことは、つまり水面下でJR東日本と前述のベンダーらが進めている開発の完了目処がたち、「2028年春より前」のタイミングでOSアップデートの形で提供されることを意味する。これは筆者の推測だが、来年2027年夏のWWDCか秋のiPhone発表会のタイミングでAppleからは何らかのアナウンスが行われるとみられ、特に新技術が世界でも有数の鉄道会社であるJR東日本によって採用されるという点で、新OSの大きなアピールポイントとして喧伝されるのではないかと考えている。
なお、中国の深センで導入されているケースを除けば、鉄道会社でのUWBウォークスルー改札は世界的にみてもまだ実証実験の段階でとどまっており、もしJR東日本がスケジュール通り2028年春に本格サービスを開始できれば、世界に先駆けての広域導入事例となる。

11/10 14:08 · 🔖 16
📄 本文を読む
無料のプロジェクト管理ツール「Leantime」、カンバン・一覧表・ガントビュー・iCal・タイムブロッキング・ホワイトボード・タイムシートを組み合わせて認知負荷を軽減することでADHD・失読症・自閉症を念頭に置いて設計
仕事や個人のプロジェクトを進める際はタスクの整理・スケジュールの確認・作業時間の記録などに複数のアプリを使い分けることがあります。そんな作業を1カ所にまとめて管理できるのが無料のオープンソースツール「Leantime」です。カンバン・一覧表・ガントビュー・カレンダー・タイムシートを備えるほか、ADHD・失読症・自閉スペクトラム症などを念頭に置いた設計も特徴とのことで実際に使ってみました。
Leantime - Open Source Project Management Software
https://leantime.io/
Leantime/leantime: Leantime is a goals focused project management system for non-project managers. Building with ADHD, Autism, and dyslexia in mind.
https://github.com/leantime/leantime
◆Leantimeの機能
・プロジェクトとタスクをまとめて管理
Leantimeはプロジェクト単位で作業を管理でき、「マイワーク」では自分に割り当てられたタスクや日々の予定をまとめて確認できます。
「カンバン表示」ではタスクがカードとして並びます。
カンバンは作業状態ごとに列を分ける表示方法で、カードを「新規」から「進行中」へ移動するだけで進捗を更新できます。
同じタスクを「テーブル表示」に切り替えると一覧表になり、担当者・期限・優先順位といった項目を見比べながら作業内容を整理できます。
・ガントビューで全体の予定を確認
マイルストーンには「調査完了」「公開準備完了」といった節目を設定でき、タイムラインでは期間を横棒で示して工程の前後関係を視覚的に確認できます。
・目標と作業を関連付ける
これらの節目は達成したい目標にも結び付けられ、単にタスクを消化するだけでなく「何のために進める作業なのか」を意識して管理できる構成です。
・タイムブロッキングで作業時間を確保
個人のカレンダーではタスクの締め切りとは別に作業予定の開始・終了時刻を設定でき、16時から17時まで作業を行うといった時間の割り当てが可能です。このように作業時間を予定として先に確保する方法は「タイムブロッキング」と呼ばれ、ほかの予定との重なりを見ながら一日の計画を具体化できます。
ユーザー別のiCal URLを外部カレンダーへ登録するとLeantime側のタスクや予定を購読できます。
・作業時間をタイムシートに記録
作業を始めるときはタスクの3点メニューから「作業開始」を選んで計測を開始。
作業が完了し「停止」アイコンをクリックすると…
経過時間が実績として記録されます。
記録した時間は「タイムシート」で日付やタスクごとに確認でき、タイマーを使う代わりに作業時間を手動で入力して週単位で集計することもできます。
・その他の機能
ほかにもWiki・アイデアボード・SWOT分析・振り返り・二要素認証などを備え、自由配置の「ホワイトボード」は別途購入する拡張プラグインとして提供されています。
・ADHD・失読症・自閉スペクトラム症を念頭に置いた設計
LeantimeはADHD・失読症・自閉スペクトラム症など認知特性の違いを考慮した「認知アクセシビリティ」を設計方針としており、仕事への集中・情報の把握・作業の進め方を支援する機能を備えています。
例えばADHDでは大きな作業に取りかかることや優先順位を決めることが負担になる場合がありますが、Leantimeではタスクを小さなサブタスクに分割し、実際に取り組む作業を絞り込めます。
失読症への配慮としては文字の形を区別しやすくすることを目指した「OpenDyslexic」フォントを利用するテーマや、文章だけでなく図やカードを使って作業を視覚的に整理する仕組みが挙げられます。
自閉スペクトラム症などへの配慮としてはメニューや操作方法を統一し、各画面で必要な情報を中心に表示することで情報の切り替えに伴う負担を軽減する設計が採用されています。
◆Leantimeの構築方法
今回はWindowsにDocker DesktopとGit for WindowsのGit Bashを用意した環境で構築します。作業フォルダーにリポジトリをクローンして「docker-leantime」フォルダーに移動し「sample.env」を「.env」としてコピー。
git clone https://github.com/Leantime/docker-leantime.git
cd docker-leantime
cp sample.env .env
「.env」ファイルをノートパッドなどで開き、データベースのパスワードとセッション用の秘密文字列を変更し、言語を「ja-JP」、タイムゾーンを「Asia/Tokyo」に設定。
LEAN_LANGUAGE = 'ja-JP'
LEAN_DEFAULT_TIMEZONE = 'Asia/Tokyo'
以下のコマンドでコンテナを起動します。
docker compose up -d
コンテナの起動後はブラウザで「http://localhost:8080/install」にアクセスし、管理者アカウントの作成フォームが表示されるので「ログイン情報」にメールアドレス、ユーザー情報として「名」に名前、「姓」に名字、「会社名」に会社名を記入して「インストール」をクリック。
アカウントの詳細の設定フォームの「名前」に名前、「役割または役職」に役割または役職、「パスワード」にパスワードを入力して「次へ」をクリック。
表示環境の設定では「文字の太さ」や「フォント」を選択し「次へ」をクリック。
さらに「カラーモード」と「配色」を選択して「次へ」をクリック。
「仕事の開始時間」「昼休憩」「仕事の終了時間」を選択し「次へ」をクリック。
最後に「登録を完了」をクリック。
Leantimeの初期画面が表示されれば構築完了です。
◆Leantimeの評価
ソーシャル系ニュースサイトのHacker NewsではLeantimeについて議論が行われており「タスク1件の表示に約2秒かかるのは遅い」という指摘に対して開発者は「約2秒という時間にはブラウザとサーバー間の通信やJavaScriptの実行時間も含まれており、PHPとデータベースの処理時間は95パーセンタイルで120.9ミリ秒、99パーセンタイルで634.11ミリ秒だった」と説明しています。一方「PHPとMySQLだけでセルフホストできるシンプルな構成が気に入った」という評価も投稿されています。

Scrum Alliance認定研修 直近開催のご案内
【資料公開】「自分の仕事はここまで」はどこから来るのか
みなさんこんにちは。@ryuzeeです。
2026年10月22日に、あるお客様の社内イベントで「『自分の仕事はここまで』はどこから来るのか」というテーマで講演したので、資料を公開します（講演依頼はお気軽にどうぞ！）。
仕事柄いろいろな組織を見ていますが、「担当外のことには手を出さない」「チケットに書いてあるとおりにやりました」「言われていないので」といった声はどこでもよく聞きます。そして決まって「あの人は主体性がない」「当事者意識が足りない」と、本人の問題として片づけられます。対策も「越境しよう」という呼びかけや研修、目標管理シートへの「主体性」の記入など、本人に向けたものばかりです。でも、呼びかけで変わるくらいなら、とっくに変わっています。本人のやる気や性格のせいにするのは、たいてい見当違いです（他人の行動の原因を状況より性格や意欲に求めすぎるのは、社会心理学で「根本的な帰属の誤り」と呼ばれる典型的な思い込みです）。
「自分の仕事はここまで」は本人の意識の問題ではなく、目標設定や評価制度、意思決定の仕組みといった構造が生み出している症状です。 線を引いている人は、その環境に正しく適応しているだけです。
今回の資料では、線を引かせている構造を心理学や組織論の知見をもとに整理し、それを踏まえてメンバーとリーダー・マネージャーがそれぞれ明日から何をすればよいかを解説しています。
参考になれば幸いです。
忙しい人向けのサマリー
「自分の仕事はここまで」は原因ではなく症状
- 「越境しよう」「当事者意識を持とう」と呼びかけても、たいてい変わらない
- 本人の意識の問題として扱うと、症状のモグラ叩きになる
- 行動は人と環境の両方で決まる（レヴィンの式）。他人の行動の原因を性格や意欲に求めすぎる傾向（根本的な帰属の誤り）にも注意する
自律は放任ではない
- アジャイルソフトウェア開発宣言の背後にある原則もスクラムガイドも、自分の仕事に線を引かない自律的なチームを前提にしている
- 自律とは、目指す方向や守るべき制約を共有したうえで、その枠の中で自分たちで判断して行動すること
- 枠がわからないと、人は安全な範囲にこもりやすくなる
- 目的が見えている、決めてよい範囲がはっきりしている、必要な情報が届く、自分の行動が結果を変えると感じられる、越境して失敗しても責められない。どれかが欠けると、人は線の内側にこもる
線の内側にこもると何が起きるか
- 指示待ち、進捗率での報告、誰も決めない合議制、チケットの投げ合い、組織の沈黙、縦割りがそのままシステムの形になる（コンウェイの法則）
- 「指示待ちをやめよう」とルールを作っても、別の形の症状が出るだけ。原因のほうを見る
線を引かせている3つの要因
- 目標設定と評価制度：目標を担当範囲まで分解すると、目標に書いていないことはする理由がなくなる。担当外の貢献は評価されにくく、全体がうまくいかなかったときは責められる。この条件なら線の内側にいるのが合理的な選択になる
- 学習性無力感：2016年の見直しで、学習しているのは「無力さ」ではなく「自分の行動で状況をコントロールできる」ということのほうだとわかった。「主体的になれ」と言うより、自分の行動で何かが変わった経験を作る
- 意思決定者の曖昧さ：誰が決めるのかわからないと、意見を出すことが「他人の領分を侵す」行為になる。承認者が複数いると責任が薄まり（責任の拡散）、決定が後から覆されると決めることそのものに意味を感じなくなる
影響力を実感できる関わり方
- 自分の意見や行動が結果に影響しないと感じると、関心を持ち続けるのは難しい
- 関心を求める前に、影響力を実感できるようにする。採用されなくてもよいので、検討されて、採否の理由が返ってくることが大切
- DACIで決める人（Approver）を1人に決め、意見を出す人（Contributor）を明確にすれば、意見を出すのは越境ではなく役割になる
- DACIの詳細は、ぼくが翻訳した書籍『Aligned ―プロダクト開発におけるステークホルダーとの関係性の築き方』を参照
線は引いてはいけないのか
- 引いてよい。チームの認知負荷には限界があるので境界は必要（『チームトポロジー』）。問題は境界があることではなく、またぎ方が決まっていないこと
- 線を引くだけで「内と外」が生まれるので、線をまたいで共有する上位の目標をセットで持つ。プロダクトゴールがこの上位目標になる
- 線を引くのは説明責任と権限まで。関心まで線を引かない
明日から試すこと
- メンバーとして
- 自分のチームのゴールを1文で説明できるか確かめる
- 自分の仕事に関わるステークホルダーを3人書き出す
- 他チームのスプリントレビューに1回出てみる
- 担当外で気になったことを1つ口に出す
- 小さく越境して、結果が返ってきた経験を記録しておく
- リーダー・マネージャーとして
- 越境して失敗した人を責めない。悪い知らせを持ってきた人に感謝する
- 提案には必ず結果を返す。通らなかったなら理由も返す
- 決める人を明確にし、承認者は1人にする
- 個人の目標とチームの成果をつなげ、担当外の貢献を見える場に出す
- チームに任せる範囲を言語化する
それでは。
